```python
def count_valid_merges(x_sub, y_sub):
    n, m = len(x_sub), len(y_sub)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to merge first i chars of x_sub and first j chars of y_sub
    # such that the last character in the merge is last_char
    # If no character yet, last_char is None
    
    # We'll use a dictionary for each state
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # dp[i][j] stores mapping from last_char to count
    dp = {}
    dp[(0, 0)] = {None: 1}
    
    for i in range(n + 1):
        for j in range(m + 1):
            if (i, j) == (0, 0):
                continue
                
            new_dp = defaultdict(int)
            
            # Process all previous states
            for prev_i in range(i + 1):
                for prev_j in range(j + 1):
                    if prev_i > i or prev_j > j:
                        continue
                    if (prev_i, prev_j) not in dp:
                        continue
                        
                    for last_char, cnt in dp[(prev_i, prev_j)].items():
                        # Try to extend with x
                        if prev_i < i and (prev_i, prev_j) != (i, j):
                            # Taking from x at position prev_i
                            char_x = x_sub[prev_i]
                            new_char = char_x
                            
                            # Check if this would create consecutive duplicates
                            if last_char is None or new_char != last_char:
                                new_dp[(prev_i + 1, prev_j)][new_char] = (new_dp[(prev_i + 1, prev_j)][new_char] + cnt) % MOD
                        
                        # Try to extend with y
                        if prev_j < j and (prev_i, prev_j) != (i, j):
                            char_y = y_sub[prev_j]
                            new_char = char_y
                            
                            if last_char is None or new_char != last_char:
                                new_dp[(prev_i, prev_j + 1)][new_char] = (new_dp[(prev_i, prev_j + 1)][new_char] + cnt) % MOD
            
            dp[(i, j)] = new_dp
    
    # Return total count at end state
    return sum(dp[(n, m)].values()) % MOD if (n, m) in dp else 0
```

```python
    if len(x_sub) == 0 and len(y_sub) == 0:
        return 1
    
    n, m = len(x_sub), len(y_sub)
```

This is still not quite right - I'm processing states multiple times. Let me rewrite with a clearer DP:

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each substring of x
for i1 in range(n):
    for j1 in range(i1, n):
        x_sub = x[i1:j1+1]
        # For each substring of y
        for i2 in range(m):
            for j2 in range(i2, m):
                y_sub = y[i2:j2+1]
                
                # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                count = count_valid_merges(x_sub, y_sub)
                total = (total + count) % MOD

print(total)

def count_valid_merges(x_sub, y_sub):
    n, m = len(x_sub), len(y_sub)
    
    # If both empty, one valid sequence (empty merge)
    if n == 0 and m == 0:
        return 1
    
    # dp[i][j] is a dictionary mapping last_char -> count
    # We'll use a 2D DP array of dictionaries
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # Initialize
    dp = [[defaultdict(int) for _ in range(m + 1)] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    # Base case: empty string
    dp[0][0][None] = 1
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for i in range(n + 1):
        for j in range(m + 1):
            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                continue
                
            # Check if we can come from (i-1, j)
            if i > 0:
                for last_char, cnt in dp[i-1][j].items():
                    char_x = x_sub[i-1]
                    if last_char is None or char_x != last_char:
                        dp[i][j][char_x] = (dp[i][j][char_x] + cnt) % MOD
            
            # Check if we can come from (i, j-1)
            if j > 0:
                for last_char, cnt in dp[i][j-1].items():
                    char_y = y_sub[j-1]
                    if last_char is None or char_y != last_char:
                        dp[i][j][char_y] = (dp[i][j][char_y] + cnt) % MOD
    
    # Sum all possibilities at the end
    return sum(dp[n][m].values()) % MOD
```

```python
def count_valid_merges(x_sub, y_sub):
    n, m = len(x_sub), len(y_sub)
    
    # If both empty, one valid sequence (empty merge)
    if n == 0 and m == 0:
        return 1
    
    # dp[i][j] is a dictionary mapping last_char -> count
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # Initialize with empty state
    dp = [[defaultdict(int) for _ in range(m + 1)] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    dp[0][0][None] = 1
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for i in range(n + 1):
        for j in range(m + 1):
            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                continue
                
            # Check if we can come from (i-1, j) - take from x
            if i > 0:
                for last_char, cnt in dp[i-1][j].items():
                    char_x = x_sub[i-1]
                    if last_char is None or char_x != last_char:
                        dp[i][j][char_x] = (dp[i][j][char_x] + cnt) % MOD
            
            # Check if we can come from (i, j-1) - take from y
            if j > 0:
                for last_char, cnt in dp[i][j-1].items():
                    char_y = y_sub[j-1]
                    if last_char is None or char_y != last_char:
                        dp[i][j][char_y] = (dp[i][j][char_y] + cnt) % MOD
    
    # Sum all possibilities at the end state
    return sum(dp[n][m].values()) % MOD
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Examples

aaa
bb


Output

forces


Output

Input

aaa


Output


0

justamassivetesttocheck
howwellyouhandlemodulooperations

Note

In the first example there are: 

  * 6 pairs of substrings "a" and "b", each with valid merging sequences "01" and "10"; 
  * 3 pairs of substrings "a" and "bb", each with a valid merging sequence "101"; 
  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of both strings.
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count the number of "merging sequences" that produce a "chaotic" merge.
3. A merging sequence consists of 0s and 1s where 0 means take from the start of x, 1 means take from the start of y.
4. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same.
5. We need to sum up f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substring pairs.

## Key Insights

1. For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, no two consecutive characters in the resulting string can be the same.
2. This means if we have a sequence of operations, the resulting characters must alternate between characters from x and y.
3. For a merge to be chaotic, we need that when we take characters from x and y alternately, we never get two identical characters back-to-back.

## Strategy

The key insight is that for a merge to be chaotic:
- If we take a character from x, the next character must come from y (to avoid same character).
- If we take a character from y, the next character must come from x (to avoid same character).

This means the merging sequence must alternate between 0 and 1.

For a valid chaotic merge:
1. If the substring lengths are different, the sequence must alternate starting from whichever has the shorter length.
2. The alternating pattern is forced: 0101... or 1010...

However, there's a constraint: even with alternating operations, if we extract the same character twice in a row from the same string, it might violate the chaotic condition.

- No two consecutive characters in the merge result can be the same.

- If we have consecutive 1s in the merging sequence, the characters we extract from y must be different.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for i2 in range(i1, n):
            x_sub = x[i1:i2+1]
            x_len = len(x_sub)
            
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for j1 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(j1, m):
                    y_sub = y[j1:j2+1]
                    y_len = len(y_sub)
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences that produce chaotic merges
                    count = 0
                    
                    # For alternating sequences, we need to check the pattern
                    # Pattern 1: start with x, then alternate
                    # Pattern 2: start with y, then alternate
                    # Only valid if the alternating sequence can use exactly x_len and y_len zeros and ones
                    
                    # For alternating pattern to work, we need |x_len - y_len| <= 1
                    if abs(x_len - y_len) > 1:
                        continue
                        
                    # Pattern 1: start with x
                    if x_len == y_len:
                        # Can start with x or with y
                        # Start with x: 0,1,0,1,...
                        if can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start='x'):
                            count += 1
                        # Start with y: 1,0,1,0,...
                        if can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start='y'):
                            count += 1
                    elif x_len > y_len:
                        # Must start with x
                        if can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start='x'):
                            count += 1
                    else:  # y_len > x_len
                        # Must start with y
                        if can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start='y'):
                            count += 1
                    
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

def can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start):
    # Build the merge sequence
    result = []
    pos_x = 0
    pos_y = 0
    
    if start == 'x':
        # 0,1,0,1,...
        for i in range(x_len + y_len):
            if i % 2 == 0:
                if pos_x < x_len:
                    result.append(x_sub[pos_x])
                    pos_x += 1
                else:
                    return False  # Shouldn't happen
            else:
                if pos_y < y_len:
                    result.append(y_sub[pos_y])
                    pos_y += 1
                else:
                    return False  # Shouldn't happen
    else:  # start == 'y'
        # 1,0,1,0,...
        for i in range(x_len + y_len):
            if i % 2 == 0:
                if pos_y < y_len:
                    result.append(y_sub[pos_y])
                    pos_y += 1
                else:
                    return False  # Shouldn't happen
            else:
                if pos_x < x_len:
                    result.append(x_sub[pos_x])
                    pos_x += 1
                else:
                    return False  # Shouldn't happen
    
    # Check if the result is chaotic: no consecutive identical characters
    for i in range(1, len(result)):
        if result[i] == result[i-1]:
            return False
    
    return True

solve()
```

In the alternating pattern, for an even index i, we take from x (if start with x), for odd index i, from y.

But in the sequence, the first character is from x, then from y, then from x, etc.

The resulting string is the sequence of characters.

For the merge to be chaotic, we need that for each i from 1 to len-1, result[i] != result[i-1].

In the above code, I'm building the result and checking that.

```python
def can_form_chaotic(x_sub, y_sub, x_len, y_len, start):
    # Build the merge sequence
    result = []
    pos_x = 0
    pos_y = 0
    
    # Construct the sequence based on start
    for i in range(x_len + y_len):
        if start == 'x':
            if i % 2 == 0:
                if pos_x < x_len:
                    result.append(x_sub[pos_x])

                else:
                    return False
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Examples

aaa
bb


Output


24

code
forces


Output

Input


aaaaa
aaa


Output


0

justamassivetesttocheck
howwellyouhandlemodulooperations

Note

In the first example there are: 

  * 6 pairs of substrings "a" and "b", each with valid merging sequences "01" and "10"; 
  * 3 pairs of substrings "a" and "bb", each with a valid merging sequence "101"; 
  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of x and y.
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count how many "merging sequences" produce a "chaotic" merge.
3. A merging sequence is a sequence of 0s and 1s where 0 means taking from x and 1 means taking from y.
4. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same.
5. We need to sum the counts over all possible substring pairs.

## Key Insights

1. For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, no two consecutive operations can be the same (no "00" or "11").
2. The merge sequence is chaotic if and only if consecutive characters in the result are different.
3. Since we're taking characters from the beginning of x or y, the merge is basically interleaving characters from both substrings.
4. For a chaotic merge, we need that consecutive characters in the result are different.

## Analysis of Chaotic Condition

For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge:
- If we take a character from x, it must be different from the next character (whether from x or y)
- If we take a character from y, it must be different from the next character

So the condition is: the character we take must be different from the subsequent character in the merge.

Actually, let me think about this more carefully:

The merge z is chaotic if z[i-1] ≠ z[i] for all i from 2 to |z|.

So for each position in the merge, the character must be different from the next character.

This means that if we have a sequence of operations, the resulting characters must alternate.

## Key Observation

For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, we need that no two consecutive characters in the result are the same.

This happens if and only if the operation sequence alternates between taking from x and y.

Actually, let's reconsider: the alternation happens in the operation sequence, not necessarily in the character values.

Wait, let me think with the example:
- x = "aaa", y = "bb"
- Pair "a" and "b": merging sequence "01" produces "ab" - a ≠ b, so chaotic. 
- Pair "a" and "bb": merging sequence "101" produces "b" + "a" + "b" = "bab" - b ≠ a, a ≠ b, so chaotic.
- Pair "aa" and "b": merging sequence "010" produces "a" + "b" + "a" = "aba" - a ≠ b, b ≠ a, so chaotic.

So the condition is that the resulting string has no consecutive identical characters.

## Solution Approach

Since the constraints are small (|x|, |y| ≤ 1000), we can use dynamic programming.

For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we can use DP to count valid merging sequences.

But we need to consider all pairs of substrings. This seems expensive.

Alternative approach: we can use a 4D DP where:
- dp[i][j][k][l] = number of ways to merge first i characters of substring from x and first j characters of substring from y such that the last character in merge is from x or y.

Wait, but we need to consider all substring pairs.

Actually, let me think about the state:

For a fixed pair of substrings, we can use:
- dp[pos_x][pos_y][last_char] = number of ways to merge x[0:pos_x] and y[0:pos_y] ending with last_char (0 for from x, 1 for from y)

The transition:
- If we take from x: dp[pos_x+1][pos_y][0] += dp[pos_x][pos_y][last_char] if (last_char == 1 or last_char doesn't exist) and the character from x is different from the character that would be at the end
- Actually, we need to ensure the merge is chaotic.

Wait, the chaotic condition is on the resulting string, not on the operation sequence. We need to ensure that consecutive characters in the merge are different.

So we need to track:
- Current position in x
- Current position in y  
- Last character in the merge (or None if at start)

The state: dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char can be 0, 1, or -1 (no previous character)

When adding a character from x[i], we check if it's different from last_char (if last_char exists).

## Implementation Strategy

1. For each possible substring of x and each possible substring of y:
   - Use DP to count valid merging sequences
2. Sum all counts

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve(x, y):
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Get substrings
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    count = count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
                    
    return total

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    # dp[i][j][last] where:
    # i = position in sub_x (0 to len(sub_x))
    # j = position in sub_y (0 to len(sub_y))
    # last = last character in merge, -1 for none, 0 for from x, 1 for from y
    
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # Initialize DP
    # dp[i][j][last] = number of ways
    dp = {}
    
    # Start with empty merge
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last in [-1, 0, 1]:
                if (i, j, last) not in dp:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last)]
                if ways == 0:
                    continue
                    
                # Try taking from x if available
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    # Check if this creates a valid transition
                    if last == -1:
                        # First character - always valid
                        new_state = (i + 1, j, 0)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Last character was from x or y
                        if last == 0:
                            # Last char was from x, so we need char_x != char_from_last
                            # But we only know last char value, not which string
                            # Actually, if last == 0, last character was from x
                            # So we need char_x != sub_x[last_pos]
                            # But we don't have last_pos, we only have last = 0
                            
                            # Problem: we need to track what the last character actually is
                            pass
                
    # Let me fix the DP state to track the actual last character value
    
    # Better DP state: dp[i][j][prev_char] where prev_char is the actual character from previous position
    # But we don't need to store position, only the character
    
    # New DP: dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is the character of the last added element
    # Or we can use last_char = -1 for no character
    
    dp = {}
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1, 0, 1]:
                if (i, j, last_char) not in dp or dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Try taking from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - valid
                        new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if char_x != last_char
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                
                # Try taking from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - valid
                        new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if char_y != last_char
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all final states
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1, 0, 1]:
        result = (result + dp.get((len_x, len_y, last_char), 0)) % MOD
    
    return result

# Read input
x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

print(solve(x, y))
```

But the state `dp[i][j][last_char]` has `last_char` as the actual character of the last added element.

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve(x, y):
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y

                    total = (total + count) % MOD

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char]: number of ways to merge first i chars of sub_x and first j chars of sub_y
    # last_char is the actual character of the last added element, or None (-1)
    dp = {}
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1, chr(ord('a'))]:
                # Better: use -1 for none, and actual characters
                pass
    
    # Let me fix: use -1 for no last character, otherwise use actual character
    dp = {}
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1] + [c for c in set(sub_x + sub_y)]:
                if (i, j, last_char) not in dp or dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Try taking from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - always valid
                        new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if char_x != last_char
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                
                # Try taking from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - always valid
                        new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if char_y != last_char
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all final states
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1] + [c for c in set(sub_x + sub_y)]:
        if (len_x, len_y, last_char) in dp:
            result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, last_char)]) % MOD
            
    return result
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve(x, y):
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Get substrings
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    count = count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
                    
    return total

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is the actual character or -1 for none
    # To avoid state explosion, we can use a dictionary
    from collections import defaultdict
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1] + [c for c in set(sub_x + sub_y)]:
                if dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Try taking from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Try taking from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all final states
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1] + [c for c in set(sub_x + sub_y)]:
        if (len_x, len_y, last_char) in dp:
            result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, last_char)]) % MOD
            
    return result
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    count = count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
                    
    print(total)

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways
    # last_char: -1 (no last char), or actual character
    from collections import defaultdict
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    # Iterate through all states
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1]:
                # First, process the state
                if dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Try taking from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - always valid
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if x char is different from last char
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Try taking from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                    if last_char == -1:
                        # First character - always valid
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Check if y char is different from last char
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all ways to fully merge
    result = 0
    # We need to consider all possible last characters
    # The last state is (len_x, len_y, last_char) for some last_char
    for last_char in ['a', 'b', 'c', 'd', 'e', 'f', 'g', 'h', 'i', 'j', 'k', 'l', 'm', 'n', 'o', 'p', 'q', 'r', 's', 't', 'u', 'v', 'w', 'x', 'y', 'z']:
        if (len_x, len_y, last_char) in dp:
            result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, last_char)]) % MOD
            
    # Also consider -1 as last_char
    if (len_x, len_y, -1) in dp:
        result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, -1)]) % MOD
        
    return result

main()
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    count = count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
```

```python
def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is -1 (none) or character
    # Use set of characters that appear in sub_x or sub_y
    chars = set(sub_x + sub_y)
    char_set = set()
    for c in sub_x:
        char_set.add(c)
    for c in sub_y:
        char_set.add(c)
    
    # Initialize DP
    from collections import defaultdict
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    # Fill DP table
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1] + list(char_set):
                if dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Take from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    new_state = (i + 1, j, char_x)
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    new_state = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            dp[new_state] = (dp[new_state] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all ways to reach full merge
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1] + list(char_set):
        result = (result + dp.get((len_x, len_y, last_char), 0)) % MOD
        
    return result
```

I think this should work. Let me test with the examples.

Actually, I see a bug - the char_set should be built from the substrings, and we're using it for the DP state.

Let me submit this solution:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    count = count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
                    
    print(total)

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # Get all unique characters in the substrings
    chars = set(sub_x) | set(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is -1 or a character in chars
    from collections import defaultdict
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1] + list(chars):
                if dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Take from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] = (dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] = (dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] = (dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] = (dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all valid final states
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1] + list(chars):
        result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, last_char)]) % MOD
        
    return result

main()
```

    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0

                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]

                    total = (total + count) % MOD
                    
    print(total)

def count_chaotic_sequences(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # Get all unique characters in the substrings
    chars = set(sub_x) | set(sub_y)
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is -1 or a character in chars
    from collections import defaultdict
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            for last_char in [-1] + list(chars):
                if dp[(i, j, last_char)] == 0:
                    continue
                    
                ways = dp[(i, j, last_char)]
                
                # Take from x
                if i < len_x:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] = (dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_x != last_char:
                            dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] = (dp[(i + 1, j, char_x)] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y
                if j < len_y:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1:
                        dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] = (dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] + ways) % MOD
                    else:
                        if char_y != last_char:
                            dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] = (dp[(i, j + 1, char_y)] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all valid final states
    result = 0
    for last_char in [-1] + list(chars):
        result = (result + dp[(len_x, len_y, last_char)]) % MOD
        
    return result

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

I think a better approach is:

For each pair of substrings (sub_x, sub_y):
- Use DP to count the number of ways to merge them such that the resulting string is chaotic
- State: dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to merge first i chars of sub_x and first j chars of sub_y, with last character being last_char
- But we need to track the last character to ensure no consecutive duplicates

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
            len_x = r1 - l1 + 1
            
            # For each possible substring of y  
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    len_y = r2 - l2 + 1
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    result = count_valid_merges(sub_x, sub_y)
                    total = (total + result) % MOD
    
    print(total)

def count_valid_merges(sub_x, sub_y):
    # Use DP to count valid merging sequences that produce chaotic merges
    # dp[i][j][last] where i = chars used from sub_x, j = chars used from sub_y
    # last = character that was last added, or -1 if none yet
    
    n, m = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    if n == 0 and m == 0:
        return 1 if n == 0 and m == 0 else 0
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways
    # We'll use last_char as chr(0) to chr(25) or -1 for no last
    # Use -1 to represent no character yet
    
    # Initialize with dp[0][0][0] = 1 (empty string)
    # But we need to handle the first character
    
    # Actually, we can do it differently:
    # For each state (i,j), we can go to (i+1,j) or (i,j+1)
    # If we go to (i+1,j), we add sub_x[i], if we go to (i,j+1), we add sub_y[j]
    # We need to ensure that the new character is different from the previous one
    
    # dp[i][j][prev_char] where prev_char is the character that was added last, or -1
    dp = {}
    
    # Start with no characters
    # We can go to (0,0) with no previous char
    # Actually, for the first character, it's always valid
    
    # Let's do it differently - for each state (i,j), we track the last character added
    # Initialize: at (0,0), we have no last character yet
    
    # dp[i][j] is a dictionary of last_char -> count
    dp = {}
    dp[(0,0)] = { -1: 1 }  # no last character
    
    for i in range(n + 1):
        for j in range(m + 1):
            if (i, j) not in dp:
                continue
                
            for last_char, count in dp[(i, j)].items():
                if i < n:
                    # Take from sub_x
                    new_char = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1 or new_char != last_char:
                        new_state = (i + 1, j)
                        if new_state not in dp:
                            dp[new_state] = {}
                        dp[new_state][new_char] = (dp[new_state].get(new_char, 0) + count) % MOD
                
                if j < m:
                    # Take from sub_y
                    new_char = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1 or new_char != last_char:
                        new_state = (i, j + 1)
                        if new_state not in dp:
                            dp[new_state] = {}
                        dp[new_state][new_char] = (dp[new_state].get(new_char, 0) + count) % MOD
    
    # Sum all ways at the final state
    if (n, m) in dp:
        return sum(dp[(n, m)].values()) % MOD
    else:
        return 0

solve()
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each possible substring of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
        len_x = r1 - l1 + 1

                total = (total + result) % MOD

print(total % MOD)

def count_valid_merges(sub_x, sub_y):
    n, m = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    # DP: dp[i][j][last] where i, j are indices, last is the last character added (-1 means none)
    # We'll use a dictionary to avoid unnecessary states
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # dp[i][j] is a dict: char -> count
    dp = {}
    dp[(0,0)] = { -1: 1 }  # no character yet
    
    for i in range(n + 1):
        for j in range(m + 1):
            if (i, j) not in dp:
                continue
                
            for last_char, count in dp[(i, j)].items():
                # Take from sub_x if possible
                if i < n:
                    char_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char == -1 or char_x != last_char:
                        next_state = (i + 1, j)
                        if next_state not in dp:
                            dp[next_state] = {}
                        dp[next_state][char_x] = (dp[next_state].get(char_x, 0) + count) % MOD
                
                # Take from sub_y if possible
                if j < m:
                    char_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char == -1 or char_y != last_char:
                        next_state = (i, j + 1)
                        if next_state not in dp:
                            dp[next_state] = {}
                        dp[next_state][char_y] = (dp[next_state].get(char_y, 0) + count) % MOD
    
    # Sum up all ways to reach the end
    if (n, m) in dp:
        return sum(dp[(n, m)].values()) % MOD
    else:
        return 0
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of both strings
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count the number of "merging sequences" that produce a "chaotic" merge
3. A merging sequence is a sequence of 0s and 1s where 0 means take from beginning of first substring, 1 means take from beginning of second substring
4. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
5. We need to sum f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substrings pairs

## Key Insights

1. For a merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in the result can be the same
2. This means in the merging sequence, we can never have two consecutive 0s or two consecutive 1s
3. So the merging sequence must alternate between 0 and 1
4. The validity depends on the relative lengths of the two substrings

## Analysis of Chaotic Condition

For a merge to be chaotic:
- If we have a sequence of 0s and 1s, consecutive elements must alternate
- This means the sequence must be of the form 0101... or 1010...
- So the total length is |sub_x| + |sub_y|
- The number of valid sequences depends on whether |sub_x| and |sub_y| allow alternating patterns

## Key Observation

For a chaotic merge to exist, we need to be able to alternate between taking from x and y. This is only possible if:
- The sequence starts with 0 (taking from x) and alternates, OR
- The sequence starts with 1 (taking from y) and alternates

For a sequence to alternate properly, we need to check if consecutive characters can be different in the final merge.

However, there's a critical insight: **the characters themselves don't matter for the chaotic condition** - what matters is the pattern of which string we take from each step.

The chaotic condition only applies to the sequence of characters in the result, not the merging sequence itself. But since we're only concerned about consecutive characters being different, and the characters come from two different strings, we need to ensure that when we alternate, we never get the same character in a row.

## Alternative Approach

After studying the examples, I believe the solution involves:
1. For each possible substring of x and each possible substring of y
2. Check if a chaotic merge can be formed
3. Count how many different merging sequences produce a chaotic merge

For chaotic merge to exist:
- The merging sequence must alternate between 0 and 1 (no two consecutive same values)
- This means we have two cases: starting with 0 or starting with 1

For a substring of length i from x and length j from y:
- If we start with 0, the sequence is 0101...0 (if i+j is odd) or 0101...01 (if i+j is even)
- If we start with 1, the sequence is 1010...1 (if i+j is odd) or 1010...10 (if i+j is even)

Let me implement a solution:

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for lx in range(n):
        for rx in range(lx, n):
            sub_x = x[lx:rx+1]
            len_x = rx - lx + 1
            
            # For each substring of y
            for ly in range(m):
                for ry in range(ly, m):
                    sub_y = y[ly:ry+1]
                    len_y = ry - ly + 1
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    count = 0
                    
                    # Try all possible merging sequences
                    # We use DP to count valid sequences
                    # dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to take i chars from sub_x and j from sub_y
                    # where last_char is the character at the end of the merge
                    
                    # But we need to check if consecutive characters in merge are different
                    # So we need to know what the last character was
                    
                    # Instead, let's try to count valid alternating sequences
                    # For a chaotic merge, no two consecutive chars can be equal
                    # This means at each step, the character we add must be different from the previous one
                    
                    # We can use DP: dp[i][j][last_char] where i, j are positions in sub_x and sub_y
                    # last_char is the character that was added last, or None if none
                    
                    # But the character we add depends on whether we take from x or y
                    
                    # Initialize with no characters
                    # We'll use memoization with (i, j, last_char)
                    
                    from functools import lru_cache
                    
                    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                    def dp(i, j, last_char):
                        # i: position in sub_x (0 to len_x)
                        # j: position in sub_y (0 to len_y)
                        # last_char: the character that was last added, or None if none
                        if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                            return 1 if last_char is None else 0
                        
                        result = 0
                        
                        # Try taking from x if there are more characters in x
                        if i < len_x:
                            new_char = sub_x[i]
                            # Check if this character is different from last_char
                            if last_char is None or new_char != last_char:
                                result = (result + dp(i + 1, j, new_char)) % MOD
                        
                        # Try taking from y if there are more characters in y
                        if j < len_y:
                            new_char = sub_y[j]
                            # Check if this character is different from last_char
                            if last_char is None or new_char != last_char:
                                result = (result + dp(i, j + 1, new_char)) % MOD
                        
                        return result
                    
                    count = dp(0, 0, None)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

```python

x = input().strip()

# For each substring of x

                    # i: index in sub_x (0 to len_x)
                    # j: index in sub_y (0 to len_y)
                    # last_char: the character from the previous step, or None if no previous step
                    if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                        return 1
                    
                    result = 0
                    
                    # Try taking from sub_x if possible
                    if i < len_x:
                        current_char = sub_x[i]
                        if last_char is None or current_char != last_char:
                            result = (result + dp(i + 1, j, current_char)) % MOD
                    
                    # Try taking from sub_y if possible
                    if j < len_y:
                        current_char = sub_y[j]
                        if last_char is None or current_char != last_char:
                            result = (result + dp(i, j + 1, current_char)) % MOD
                    
                    return result
                
                count = dp(0, 0, None)
                total = (total + count) % MOD

print(total)
```

This should work, though it might be borderline in performance for the constraints (1000x1000 = 1e6 substrings, each with O(length) operations). But the length of substrings is at most 1000, so worst case is too slow.

I need to optimize. The number of substrings is O(n^2 * m^2) which is 10^12 for n=m=1000, which is impossible.

There must be a simpler pattern or mathematical insight.

After more thought, perhaps the chaotic condition can be satisfied only in very specific cases, and the answer is related to alternating patterns.

Looking at the examples again:

In "aaa" and "bb":
- Substrings "a" and "b": valid (01, 10) - gives "ab" or "ba" which are chaotic
- Substrings "a" and "bb": valid (101) - gives "bab" 
- Substrings "aa" and "b": valid (010) - gives "aab" or "aba" - "aab" has "aa" which is not chaotic
Wait, "aab" has a= a, so not chaotic.

I think I need to re-examine the merge process.

Let me implement the DP solution with optimization for the state space:

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each substring of x
for lx in range(n):
    for rx in range(lx, n):
        sub_x = x[lx:rx+1]
        len_x = rx - lx + 1
        
        # For each substring of y
        for ly in range(m):
            for ry in range(ly, m):
                sub_y = y[ly:ry+1]
                len_y = ry - ly + 1
                
                # Count valid chaotic merges using DP
                # dp[i][j][last] where last is None or a char
                # Use dictionary to avoid storing impossible states
                
                from functools import lru_cache
                
                @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                def count_ways(i, j, last):
                    if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                        return 1
                    
                    ways = 0
                    
                    # Take from x
                    if i < len_x:
                        char_x = sub_x[i]
                        if last is None or char_x != last:
                            ways = (ways + count_ways(i + 1, j, char_x)) % MOD
                    
                    # Take from y
                    if j < len_y:
                        char_y = sub_y[j]
                        if last is None or char_y != last:
                            ways = (ways + count_ways(i, j + 1, char_y)) % MOD
                    
                    return ways
                
                result = count_ways(0, 0, None)
                total = (total + result) % MOD

print(total)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Output

aaa


Output

justamassivetesttocheck
howwellyouhandlemodulooperations

Note

In the first example there are: 

  * 6 pairs of substrings "a" and "b", each with valid merging sequences "01" and "10"; 
  * 3 pairs of substrings "a" and "bb", each with a valid merging sequence "101"; 
  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of x and y.
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count the number of "chaotic" merging sequences.
3. A merging sequence produces a string z where:
   - 0 means take from the beginning of x's substring
   - 1 means take from the beginning of y's substring
4. A merge is "chaotic" if no two consecutive characters are the same (z[i-1] ≠ z[i]).
5. We need to sum f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substring pairs.

## Key Insights

1. For a merging sequence to be chaotic, no two consecutive operations can be the same. This means we can't have "00" or "11" in the sequence.

2. For a given pair of substrings of lengths m and n, we need to count valid interleaving sequences where no two consecutive elements are the same.

3. The problem can be solved using dynamic programming. We need to track:
   - Current position in x's substring
   - Current position in y's substring  
   - The last operation performed (0 or 1) to ensure chaos

4. For each valid interleaving, we need to check if the sequence is chaotic (no consecutive identical operations).

## Algorithm

1. For each possible substring of x and each possible substring of y:
   - Use DP to count valid chaotic merging sequences
   - State: dp[i][j][last] where:
     - i = current position in x's substring
     - j = current position in y's substring  
     - last = last operation (0 or 1, or -1 for start)

2. For each valid sequence, we need to ensure no consecutive same operations.

3. Sum the results for all substring pairs.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total_sum = 0
    
    # Try all possible substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Try all possible substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Calculate f(l1, r1, l2, r2) using DP
                    len_x = r1 - l1 + 1
                    len_y = r2 - l2 + 1
                    
                    # DP[i][j][last] = number of ways to process first i chars of x
                    # and j chars of y, with last operation being 'last'
                    # last: 0 means last was from x, 1 means last was from y, -1 means start
                    # We need to handle the case where we haven't made any operation yet
                    
                    # Initialize DP table
                    # dp[i][j][k] where k = -1, 0, 1
                    # We'll use a 3D array with dimensions (len_x+1) x (len_y+1) x 3
                    dp = [[[0] * 3 for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
                    
                    # Base case: at start, no operations made
                    dp[0][0][0] = 1  # last operation is "start" (represented as 0)
                    
                    # Fill the DP table
                    for i in range(len_x + 1):
                        for j in range(len_y + 1):
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                                
                            # Case 1: take from x (if we have chars left)
                            if i > 0:
                                # If we just came from operation 1 (from y), we can add 0
                                if j > 0 and dp[i-1][j][1] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][1]) % MOD
                                # If we just came from operation 0 (from x), we cannot add 0 (not chaotic)
                                # If we just came from start, we can add 0
                                if dp[i-1][j][0] > 0:
                                    # This would create "00" which is invalid
                                    pass
                                if dp[i-1][j][0] > 0 or dp[i-1][j][2] > 0:  # start case
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][0]) % MOD
                                
                                # Actually, let me restructure: we don't want to add from same operation
                                # We'll build it properly
                                
                    # Let me restart the DP with proper state management
                    dp = [[[0] * 3 for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
                    
                    # dp[i][j][0] = ways to reach (i,j) with last operation from x (0)
                    # dp[i][j][1] = ways to reach (i,j) with last operation from y (1)
                    # dp[i][j][2] = ways to reach (i,j) with no previous operation (start)
                    
                    dp[0][0][2] = 1  # start state
                    
                    for i in range(len_x + 1):
                        for j in range(len_y + 1):
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                                
                            # From x
                            if i > 0:
                                # We can come from j, with previous operation being y (1) or start (2)
                                if j < len_y and dp[i-1][j][1] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][1]) % MOD
                                if j < len_y and dp[i-1][j][2] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][2]) % MOD
                                
                            # From y  
                            if j > 0:
                                if i < len_x and dp[i][j-1][0] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][1] = (dp[i][j][1] + dp[i][j-1][0]) % MOD
                                if i < len_x and dp[i][j-1][2] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][1] = (dp[i][j][1] + dp[i][j-1][2]) % MOD
                    
                    # Sum all valid sequences
                    result = (dp[len_x][len_y][0] + dp[len_x][len_y][1]) % MOD
                    total_sum = (total_sum + result) % MOD
    
    print(total_sum)

solve()
```

Actually, let me simplify and fix the algorithm:

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total_sum = 0
    
    # Try all possible substrings of x and y
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    len_x = r1 - l1 + 1
                    len_y = r2 - l2 + 1
                    
                    # dp[i][j][last] where last is 0 (from x), 1 (from y), or -1 (start)
                    # We'll use 3 states: 0, 1, 2 where 2 is start
                    dp = [[[0] * 3 for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
                    
                    # Base case: at start
                    dp[0][0][2] = 1
                    
                    # Fill the DP table
                    for i in range(len_x + 1):
                        for j in range(len_y + 1):
                            # Skip the initial state
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                                
                            # Option 1: take from x (if we have characters)
                            if i > 0:
                                # Can come from previous state with operation from y (1) or start (2)
                                # Cannot come from previous operation with x (0) because that would create "00"
                                if j < len_y and dp[i-1][j][1] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][1]) % MOD
                                if j < len_y and dp[i-1][j][2] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][0] = (dp[i][j][0] + dp[i-1][j][2]) % MOD
                                
                            # Option 2: take from y (if we have characters)
                            if j > 0:
                                # Can come from previous state with operation from x (0) or start (2)
                                # Cannot come from previous operation with y (1) because that would create "11"
                                if i < len_x and dp[i][j-1][0] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][1] = (dp[i][j][1] + dp[i][j-1][0]) % MOD
                                if i < len_x and dp[i][j-1][2] > 0:
                                    dp[i][j][1] = (dp[i][j][1] + dp[i][j-1][2]) % MOD
                    
                    # Add the number of valid chaotic sequences
                    total_sum = (total_sum + dp[len_x][len_y][0] + dp[len_x][len_y][1]) % MOD
    
    print(total_sum)

solve()
```

    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total_sum = 0

    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    len_x = r1 - l1 + 1
                    len_y = r2 - l2 + 1
                    
                    # dp[i][j][last] where last is 0 (from x), 1 (from y), or -1 (start)
                    # We'll use 3 states: 0, 1, 2 where 2 is start
                    dp = [[[0] * 3 for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
                    
                    # Base case: at start
                    dp[0][0][2] = 1
                    
                    # Fill the DP table
                    for i in range(len_x + 1):
                        for j in range(len_y + 1):
                            # Skip the initial state
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                                
                            # Option 1: take from x (if we have characters)

                                if j < len_y and dp[i-1][j][2] > 0:

                    # Add the number of valid chaotic sequences
                    total_sum = (total_sum + dp[len_x][len_y][0] + dp[len_x][len_y][1]) % MOD
